Um, yeah, that's definitely changed. I used to really look forward to my softball league, my book club, working in my garden... and the robotics club I run at school. 

Actually, robotics used to be the absolute highlight of my week. We made it to the state semi-finals two years ago, which was a massive deal because our district gives us zero funding for parts. We were up against these prep schools that had actual 3D printers and laser cutters, and my kids were basically hot-gluing servos to scrap PVC pipes we found behind the maintenance shed. It was so scrappy, but they were so dedicated. One of my students from that team, David, he actually just got a full ride to an engineering program, which is just incredible because his home life is... well, it's really rough. His mom works three jobs just to keep the lights on, so he practically lived in my classroom after school... 

Wait, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, enjoying things. Right. Um, yeah, I used to love all of that—the garden, softball, the clubs—but now I just mostly go through the motions. Or I just cancel my plans altogether because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore, to be honest.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, honestly? Everything has pretty much just started to feel unenjoyable. Like I said, I used to really love all those things—softball, the book club, my garden, the robotics club. But now... I don't know, it's just like a chore. 

Like with the garden, I have these raised beds in the backyard that I built myself a few years ago. I spent weeks hauling bags of soil and compost in the trunk of that awful Honda I mentioned. I actually tried growing heirloom tomatoes last summer, the Cherokee Purple ones? They're supposed to be amazing, but the deer in our neighborhood are just relentless. They jumped a six-foot fence like it was nothing and ate the tops off every single plant right before they ripened. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she swears by putting Irish Spring soap shavings around the perimeter, but I'm pretty sure it just made my yard smell like a men's locker room and didn't stop a single deer... 

But, um, anyway. Yeah. Nothing really brings me any pleasure anymore. I mostly just go through the motions or cancel my plans completely.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty awful lately. I can be completely exhausted, just bone-tired from the day, but I'll still lie awake for hours. I just keep replaying all these assessment deadlines in my head, or agonizing over stupid mistakes I made in the classroom. 

Like on Tuesday, I accidentally handed back the 10th graders' quizzes before I entered the grades into the online portal. Which sounds like a small thing, but the portal locks you out if you don't input the data within 48 hours of the assessment date. It's this new district policy they rolled out in January, and the IT guy, Gary—who is never actually in his office when you need him, by the way—sent this passive-aggressive all-staff email about "data compliance." I swear, Gary spends more time policing our gradebook entries than actually fixing the school's Wi-Fi, which drops out every time it rains. And it rained for three days straight last week, so half my kids couldn't even access the digital textbook...

Wait, sorry. Sleep. Right. So yeah, I lie awake worrying about all that stuff. And then, even when I do finally fall asleep, I always seem to wake up around 4 a.m., and I never really get back to sleep after that. I just lie there until my alarm goes off.

Everything has pretty much just started to feel unenjoyable. Like I said, I used to really love all those things—softball, the book club, my garden, the robotics club. But now... I don't know, it's just like a chore. \n\nLike with the garden, I have these raised beds in the backyard that I built myself a few years ago. I spent weeks hauling bags of soil and compost in the trunk of that awful Honda I mentioned. I actually tried growing heirloom tomatoes last summer, the Cherokee Purple ones? They're supposed to be amazing, but the deer in our neighborhood are just relentless. They jumped a six-foot fence like it was nothing and ate the tops off every single plant right before they ripened. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she swears by putting Irish Spring soap shavings around the perimeter, but I'm pretty sure it just made my yard smell like a men's locker room and didn't stop a single deer... \n\nBut, um, anyway. Yeah. Nothing really brings me any pleasure anymore. I mostly just go through the motions or cancel my plans completely.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty awful lately. I can be completely exhausted, just bone-tired from the day, but I'll still lie awake for hours. I just keep replaying all these assessment deadlines in my head, or agonizing over stupid mistakes I made in the classroom. \n\nLike on Tuesday, I accidentally handed back the 10th graders' quizzes before I entered the grades into the online portal. Which sounds like a small thing, but the portal locks you out if you don't input the data within 48 hours of the assessment date. It's this new district policy they rolled out in January, and the IT guy, Gary—who is never actually in his office when you need him, by the way—sent this passive-aggressive all-staff email about \"data compliance.\" I swear, Gary spends more time policing our gradebook entries than actually fixing the school's Wi-Fi, which drops out every time it rains. And it rained for three days straight last week, so half my kids couldn't even access the digital textbook...\n\nWait, sorry. Sleep. Right. So yeah, I lie awake worrying about all that stuff. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, didn't you just... sorry, didn't we just talk about this? Or maybe you asked it differently. But yeah, like I was saying before, I've definitely lost interest in basically everything. I used to really look forward to my softball league, the book club, my garden, and the robotics club. But now I mostly just go through the motions, or I just cancel on everyone. 

Like with book club, we were supposed to read this historical fiction novel set in World War II. Which is normally my favorite genre, you know? I actually minored in history in college, and my capstone project was on the French Resistance. I spent an entire semester digging through these translated primary documents, which was fascinating but also incredibly depressing. My advisor, Dr. Hastings, he was this brilliant guy who always wore these tweed jackets with the elbow patches—like a walking cliché, honestly—and he used to tell me I should go to grad school. But then I got the teaching job, and you know how that goes, you just get sucked into the day-to-day grind and the grading and the parent-teacher conferences... 

But anyway, sorry, yeah. None of the stuff I used to like feels enjoyable anymore. I just don't have the interest.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, yeah, I guess so. I mean, my clothes have definitely gotten noticeably looser over the past year, even though I haven't been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days, I just completely forget to eat, or I just avoid it until late afternoon. 

The cafeteria food at school is just so unappetizing anyway. They switched vendors back in September to save money, and now everything comes in these little plastic wrappers that are impossible to open. The kids complain about it constantly. My homeroom student, Leo—he's this really sweet kid, always wears these oversized graphic tees—he started a petition to bring back the old vendor because he said the new chicken nuggets taste like cardboard. He actually got over two hundred signatures, which is pretty impressive for a sophomore. He brought the petition to the principal, Mr. Davis, who is just... well, he's the kind of guy who uses words like "synergy" and "paradigm shift" in staff meetings but never actually listens to anyone. He just patted Leo on the shoulder and gave him some corporate speech about budget constraints and nutritional mandates. It was so dismissive. I felt so bad for the kid, he worked so hard on that...

But, um, sorry. Weight. Yeah. I'm definitely losing weight without trying, mostly because I just don't eat much during the day anymore.

And then, even when I do finally fall asleep, I always seem to wake up around 4 a.m., and I never really get back to sleep after that. I just lie there until my alarm goes off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, didn't you just... sorry, didn't we just talk about this? Or maybe you asked it differently. But yeah, like I was saying before, I've definitely lost interest in basically everything. I used to really look forward to my softball league, the book club, my garden, and the robotics club. But now I mostly just go through the motions, or I just cancel on everyone. \n\nLike with book club, we were supposed to read this historical fiction novel set in World War II. Which is normally my favorite genre, you know? I actually minored in history in college, and my capstone project was on the French Resistance. I spent an entire semester digging through these translated primary documents, which was fascinating but also incredibly depressing. My advisor, Dr. Hastings, he was this brilliant guy who always wore these tweed jackets with the elbow patches—like a walking cliché, honestly—and he used to tell me I should go to grad school. But then I got the teaching job, and you know how that goes, you just get sucked into the day-to-day grind and the grading and the parent-teacher conferences... \n\nBut anyway, sorry, yeah. None of the stuff I used to like feels enjoyable anymore. I just don't have the interest.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I guess so. I mean, my clothes have definitely gotten noticeably looser over the past year, even though I haven't been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days, I just completely forget to eat, or I just avoid it until late afternoon. \n\nThe cafeteria food at school is just so unappetizing anyway. They switched vendors back in September to save money, and now everything comes in these little plastic wrappers that are impossible to open. The kids complain about it constantly. My homeroom student, Leo—he's this really sweet kid, always wears these oversized graphic tees—he started a petition to bring back the old vendor because he said the new chicken nuggets taste like cardboard. He actually got over two hundred signatures, which is pretty impressive for a sophomore. He brought the petition to the principal, Mr. Davis, who is just... well, he's the kind of guy who uses words like \"synergy\" and \"paradigm shift\" in staff meetings but never actually listens to anyone. He just patted Leo on the shoulder and gave him some corporate speech about budget constraints and nutritional mandates. It was so dismissive. I felt so bad for the kid, he worked so hard on that...\n\nBut, um, sorry. Weight. Yeah. I'm definitely losing weight without trying, mostly because I just don't eat much during the day anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever thought about how you would end your life if you were to act on those thoughts about not waking up?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, I haven't... I haven't really thought about *how* I would do it. I don't want to actually do anything. It's just... I'm here because I've started catching myself thinking it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up. 

And sometimes, on the drive home from school, I just wonder whether my family and my students would be better off without me. Which really scares me, honestly. The drive home is always when it hits me the hardest. I take Route 9, which is usually backed up past the interstate, especially since they started that construction project near the overpass. They've had the right lane closed since October, and supposedly they're widening it to add a carpool lane, but I swear I never actually see anyone working on it. There are just all these orange cones everywhere and those big electronic signs that flash "Expect Delays." And the traffic is just crawling, so you're just sitting there, inching forward, and your mind just wanders. 

The other day, I was stuck behind this old station wagon with a bumper sticker that said "My child is an honor student," and it just made me think about my kids at school. And then I just started spiraling, thinking about how I'm failing them, and failing my family, and how maybe they'd just all be relieved if I wasn't around to be such a burden. 

But... yeah. Sorry. No, I don't have a plan or anything. It's just those thoughts that scare me.

Um, it's been a while now. I'd say about... a year and seven months? Yeah, that sounds right. It all started around the time I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching load. 

They really needed someone to step up because the district completely overhauled the curriculum standards over the summer, and our science department chair, Brenda, went on maternity leave right before the school year started. She had a little girl, actually. Named her Hazel. I saw pictures on Facebook, and she's adorable, but Brenda's leave left us in such a bind. So the principal asked me to handle the redesign. I didn't really want to, but I have a hard time saying no, especially when it comes to the kids. And the new standards are just... they're so rigid. They want everything quantifiable, but how do you quantify a kid's curiosity when they're building a robotics project? We used to have this great unit where they'd design their own water filtration systems using recycled materials, and it was so hands-on, but now we have to spend three weeks prepping them for this standardized test that doesn't even measure practical application. It's just bubbling in scantrons. 

And the meetings... oh my gosh, the endless district meetings. They're always at the central office, which is all the way across town, right next to that new shopping center they just built. Have you seen it? They put in one of those fancy grocery stores where a bell pepper costs like four dollars. I went in there once just to grab a coffee, and the line was out the door. 

But, um, anyway... yeah. A year and seven months. Ever since I took on that extra STEM project, everything just started feeling so heavy, and I lost interest in pretty much everything else.

She had a little girl, actually. Named her Hazel. I saw pictures on Facebook, and she's adorable, but Brenda's leave left us in such a bind. So the principal asked me to handle the redesign. I didn't really want to, but I have a hard time saying no, especially when it comes to the kids. And the new standards are just... they're so rigid. They want everything quantifiable, but how do you quantify a kid's curiosity when they're building a robotics project? A year and seven months. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Wait, two years? Um... well, like I just said, this whole thing has been going on for about a year and seven months. Ever since I took over that STEM assessment redesign. So... before that, yeah, I guess I was fine. I wasn't having those thoughts or feeling this... blankness. 

Before Brenda went on maternity leave, I actually really loved my job. I was running the after-school robotics club, and we were preparing for this regional competition over in the next county. It was at this huge convention center, the one they usually use for those big home and garden shows? My husband Dan and I went to one of those shows once, back when we were looking for ideas for our kitchen. They had this amazing booth with custom cabinetry made from reclaimed barn wood. The guy running the booth said he sourced all the wood from these old, dilapidated barns out in Amish country. He had this whole photo album showing the barns before they took them down. It was actually really sad, in a way, seeing these beautiful old structures just falling apart. 

But anyway, the robotics competition was there, and my kids had built this little rover that could navigate a maze and pick up ping pong balls. They were so proud of it. We had these matching team t-shirts made, bright neon green so I wouldn't lose anyone in the crowd. I still have mine folded in the bottom of my dresser drawer. I haven't worn it since... well, since everything started feeling so heavy. 

So, yeah. Before a year and seven months ago, I didn't feel like this. It really just started with that curriculum overhaul.

We used to have this great unit where they'd design their own water filtration systems using recycled materials, and it was so hands-on, but now we have to spend three weeks prepping them for this standardized test that doesn't even measure practical application. It's just bubbling in scantrons. \n\nAnd the meetings... oh my gosh, the endless district meetings. They're always at the central office, which is all the way across town, right next to that new shopping center they just built. Have you seen it? They put in one of those fancy grocery stores where a bell pepper costs like four dollars. I went in there once just to grab a coffee, and the line was out the door. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. A year and seven months. Ever since I took on that extra STEM project, everything just started feeling so heavy, and I lost interest in pretty much everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more years where you didn't feel this way, with interest in activities and without thoughts of not waking up?\n\nPatient: Wait, two years? Um... well, like I just said, this whole thing has been going on for about a year and seven months. Ever since I took over that STEM assessment redesign. So... before that, yeah, I guess I was fine. I wasn't having those thoughts or feeling this... blankness. \n\nBefore Brenda went on maternity leave, I actually really loved my job. I was running the after-school robotics club, and we were preparing for this regional competition over in the next county. It was at this huge convention center, the one they usually use for those big home and garden shows? My husband Dan and I went to one of those shows once, back when we were looking for ideas for our kitchen. They had this amazing booth with custom cabinetry made from reclaimed barn wood. The guy running the booth said he sourced all the wood from these old, dilapidated barns out in Amish country. He had this whole photo album showing the barns before they took them down. It was actually really sad, in a way, seeing these beautiful old structures just falling apart. \n\nBut anyway, the robotics competition was there, and my kids had built this little rover that could navigate a maze and pick up ping pong balls. They were so proud of it. We had these matching team t-shirts made, bright neon green so I wouldn't lose anyone in the crowd. I still have mine folded in the bottom of my dresser drawer. I haven't worn it since... well, since everything started feeling so heavy. \n\nSo, yeah. Before a year and seven months ago, I didn't feel like this. It really just started with that curriculum overhaul.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any periods of time, since this all started, where you felt completely back to normal for at least a couple of months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, completely back to normal for a couple of months? No, I don't think so. Not since it started. It's just been this constant weight. 

I mean, there are days where maybe I can fake it a little better, you know? Like when my sister came to visit for Thanksgiving. She drove all the way up from North Carolina. She brought her dog, this golden retriever mix named Buster. He is the sweetest thing, but he sheds everywhere. I swear I was finding blonde dog hair on my black work pants for weeks after they left. I had to buy one of those heavy-duty lint rollers, the ones with the really sticky tape? I found them on sale at Target. Target is so dangerous, though. You go in for a lint roller and come out with three throw pillows and a new coffee mug. They have this whole section right at the front with those dollar items, and they had these little ceramic pumpkins... 

But, um, anyway. No. Even when she was here, and we were making the turkey and everything, I was just going through the motions. I haven't had a real break from feeling like this since it all started.

Or maybe you asked it differently. But yeah, like I was saying before, I've definitely lost interest in basically everything. I used to really look forward to my softball league, the book club, my garden, and the robotics club. I haven't really thought about *how* I would do it. I don't want to actually do anything. It's just... I'm here because I've started catching myself thinking it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up. \n\nAnd sometimes, on the drive home from school, I just wonder whether my family and my students would be better off without me. Which really scares me, honestly. The drive home is always when it hits me the hardest. I take Route 9, which is usually backed up past the interstate, especially since they started that construction project near the overpass. They've had the right lane closed since October, and supposedly they're widening it to add a carpool lane, but I swear I never actually see anyone working on it. There are just all these orange cones everywhere and those big electronic signs that flash \"Expect Delays.\" And the traffic is just crawling, so you're just sitting there, inching forward, and your mind just wanders. \n\nThe other day, I was stuck behind this old station wagon with a bumper sticker that said \"My child is an honor student,\" and it just made me think about my kids at school. And then I just started spiraling, thinking about how I'm failing them, and failing my family, and how maybe they'd just all be relieved if I wasn't around to be such a burden. \n\nBut... yeah. Sorry. No, I don't have a plan or anything. It's just those thoughts that scare me.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about a specific method to end your life?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or... sorry, maybe I'm misunderstanding. He works at this logistics company out in the industrial park, and their morning shift starts brutally early. They've been making him do all this mandatory overtime lately because two of their floor managers quit in the same week. One of them moved to Florida, I think? Or maybe Texas. Somewhere warm. Dan's been so exhausted, he just comes home and crashes on the couch watching those home renovation shows. You know the ones where they take a terrible house and make it open-concept? She had a little girl, actually. Named her Hazel. I saw pictures on Facebook, and she's adorable, but Brenda's leave left us in such a bind. So the principal asked me to handle the redesign. I didn't really want to, but I have a hard time saying no, especially when it comes to the kids. And the new standards are just... they're so rigid. They want everything quantifiable, but how do you quantify a kid's curiosity when they're building a robotics project? It's just bubbling in scantrons. \n\nAnd the meetings... oh my gosh, the endless district meetings. A year and seven months. No, not really. It hasn't ever just... gone away. It's been pretty constant since that whole curriculum thing started. 

Like I said, there are days where I can push through it, especially when I'm at school and the kids are right there in front of me. You kind of have to be "on" for them, you know? I don't know, it's just like a chore. \n\nLike with the garden, I have these raised beds in the backyard that I built myself a few years ago. They're supposed to be amazing, but the deer in our neighborhood are just relentless. They jumped a six-foot fence like it was nothing and ate the tops off every single plant right before they ripened. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she swears by putting Irish Spring soap shavings around the perimeter, but I'm pretty sure it just made my yard smell like a men's locker room and didn't stop a single deer... \n\nBut, um, anyway. Yeah. Nothing really brings me any pleasure anymore. I mostly just go through the motions or cancel my plans completely.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns recently?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, my sleep has been pretty awful lately. I can be completely exhausted, just bone-tired from the day, but I'll still lie awake for hours. I just keep replaying all these assessment deadlines in my head, or agonizing over stupid mistakes I made in the classroom. \n\nLike on Tuesday, I accidentally handed back the 10th graders' quizzes before I entered the grades into the online portal. Which sounds like a small thing, but the portal locks you out if you don't input the data within 48 hours of the assessment date. It's this new district policy they rolled out in January, and the IT guy, Gary—who is never actually in his office when you need him, by the way—sent this passive-aggressive all-staff email about \"data compliance.\" I swear, Gary spends more time policing our gradebook entries than actually fixing the school's Wi-Fi, which drops out every time it rains. And it rained for three days straight last week, so half my kids couldn't even access the digital textbook...\n\nWait, sorry. Sleep. Right. So yeah, I lie awake worrying about all that stuff. And then, even when I do finally fall asleep, I always seem to wake up around 4 a.m., and I never really get back to sleep after that. I just lie there until my alarm goes off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, didn't you just... sorry, didn't we just talk about this? Or maybe you asked it differently. But yeah, like I was saying before, I've definitely lost interest in basically everything. I used to really look forward to my softball league, the book club, my garden, and the robotics club. But now I mostly just go through the motions, or I just cancel on everyone. \n\nLike with book club, we were supposed to read this historical fiction novel set in World War II. Which is normally my favorite genre, you know? I actually minored in history in college, and my capstone project was on the French Resistance. I spent an entire semester digging through these translated primary documents, which was fascinating but also incredibly depressing. My advisor, Dr. Hastings, he was this brilliant guy who always wore these tweed jackets with the elbow patches—like a walking cliché, honestly—and he used to tell me I should go to grad school. But then I got the teaching job, and you know how that goes, you just get sucked into the day-to-day grind and the grading and the parent-teacher conferences... \n\nBut anyway, sorry, yeah. None of the stuff I used to like feels enjoyable anymore. I just don't have the interest.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, I guess so. I mean, my clothes have definitely gotten noticeably looser over the past year, even though I haven't been trying to lose weight or anything. Most school days, I just completely forget to eat, or I just avoid it until late afternoon. \n\nThe cafeteria food at school is just so unappetizing anyway. They switched vendors back in September to save money, and now everything comes in these little plastic wrappers that are impossible to open. The kids complain about it constantly. My homeroom student, Leo—he's this really sweet kid, always wears these oversized graphic tees—he started a petition to bring back the old vendor because he said the new chicken nuggets taste like cardboard. He actually got over two hundred signatures, which is pretty impressive for a sophomore. He brought the petition to the principal, Mr. Davis, who is just... well, he's the kind of guy who uses words like \"synergy\" and \"paradigm shift\" in staff meetings but never actually listens to anyone. He just patted Leo on the shoulder and gave him some corporate speech about budget constraints and nutritional mandates. It was so dismissive. I felt so bad for the kid, he worked so hard on that...\n\nBut, um, sorry. Weight. Yeah. I'm definitely losing weight without trying, mostly because I just don't eat much during the day anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about how you would end your life if you were to act on those thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I haven't... I haven't really thought about *how* I would do it. I don't want to actually do anything. It's just... I'm here because I've started catching myself thinking it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up. \n\nAnd sometimes, on the drive home from school, I just wonder whether my family and my students would be better off without me. Which really scares me, honestly. The drive home is always when it hits me the hardest. I take Route 9, which is usually backed up past the interstate, especially since they started that construction project near the overpass. They've had the right lane closed since October, and supposedly they're widening it to add a carpool lane, but I swear I never actually see anyone working on it. There are just all these orange cones everywhere and those big electronic signs that flash \"Expect Delays.\" And the traffic is just crawling, so you're just sitting there, inching forward, and your mind just wanders. \n\nThe other day, I was stuck behind this old station wagon with a bumper sticker that said \"My child is an honor student,\" and it just made me think about my kids at school. And then I just started spiraling, thinking about how I'm failing them, and failing my family, and how maybe they'd just all be relieved if I wasn't around to be such a burden. \n\nBut... yeah. Sorry. No, I don't have a plan or anything. It's just those thoughts that scare me.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about a specific method to end your life?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or... sorry, maybe I'm misunderstanding. Like I was just saying a second ago, no, I don't have a specific method. I don't actually want to do anything to hurt myself, which is why I'm here. It's just those thoughts that creep in, you know? Like wishing I just wouldn't wake up in the morning. \n\nMy husband, Dan, usually wakes up before me anyway because his alarm goes off at 5:30. He works at this logistics company out in the industrial park, and their morning shift starts brutally early. They've been making him do all this mandatory overtime lately because two of their floor managers quit in the same week. One of them moved to Florida, I think? Or maybe Texas. Somewhere warm. Dan's been so exhausted, he just comes home and crashes on the couch watching those home renovation shows. You know the ones where they take a terrible house and make it open-concept? I used to love watching those with him, we even talked about knocking down the wall between our kitchen and living room. We had this whole Pinterest board going for it. \n\nBut... sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right. No method. Just... sometimes on that drive home, I get stuck thinking that Dan and my students would just be better off without me dragging them down. And it really scares me that my brain even goes there. But no, I haven't thought of a way to actually do it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with no interest in activities and thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: Um, it's been a while now. I'd say about... a year and seven months? Yeah, that sounds right. It all started around the time I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching load. \n\nThey really needed someone to step up because the district completely overhauled the curriculum standards over the summer, and our science department chair, Brenda, went on maternity leave right before the school year started. She had a little girl, actually. Named her Hazel. I saw pictures on Facebook, and she's adorable, but Brenda's leave left us in such a bind. So the principal asked me to handle the redesign. I didn't really want to, but I have a hard time saying no, especially when it comes to the kids. And the new standards are just... they're so rigid. They want everything quantifiable, but how do you quantify a kid's curiosity when they're building a robotics project? We used to have this great unit where they'd design their own water filtration systems using recycled materials, and it was so hands-on, but now we have to spend three weeks prepping them for this standardized test that doesn't even measure practical application. It's just bubbling in scantrons. \n\nAnd the meetings... oh my gosh, the endless district meetings. They're always at the central office, which is all the way across town, right next to that new shopping center they just built. Have you seen it? They put in one of those fancy grocery stores where a bell pepper costs like four dollars. I went in there once just to grab a coffee, and the line was out the door. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. A year and seven months. Ever since I took on that extra STEM project, everything just started feeling so heavy, and I lost interest in pretty much everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more years where you didn't feel this way, with interest in activities and without thoughts of not waking up?\n\nPatient: Wait, two years? Um... well, like I just said, this whole thing has been going on for about a year and seven months. Ever since I took over that STEM assessment redesign. So... before that, yeah, I guess I was fine. I wasn't having those thoughts or feeling this... blankness. \n\nBefore Brenda went on maternity leave, I actually really loved my job. I was running the after-school robotics club, and we were preparing for this regional competition over in the next county. It was at this huge convention center, the one they usually use for those big home and garden shows? My husband Dan and I went to one of those shows once, back when we were looking for ideas for our kitchen. They had this amazing booth with custom cabinetry made from reclaimed barn wood. The guy running the booth said he sourced all the wood from these old, dilapidated barns out in Amish country. He had this whole photo album showing the barns before they took them down. It was actually really sad, in a way, seeing these beautiful old structures just falling apart. \n\nBut anyway, the robotics competition was there, and my kids had built this little rover that could navigate a maze and pick up ping pong balls. They were so proud of it. We had these matching team t-shirts made, bright neon green so I wouldn't lose anyone in the crowd. I still have mine folded in the bottom of my dresser drawer. I haven't worn it since... well, since everything started feeling so heavy. \n\nSo, yeah. Before a year and seven months ago, I didn't feel like this. It really just started with that curriculum overhaul.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time, since this all started, where you felt completely back to normal for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Um, completely back to normal for a couple of months? No, I don't think so. Not since it started. It's just been this constant weight. \n\nI mean, there are days where maybe I can fake it a little better, you know? Like when my sister came to visit for Thanksgiving. She drove all the way up from North Carolina. She brought her dog, this golden retriever mix named Buster. He is the sweetest thing, but he sheds everywhere. I swear I was finding blonde dog hair on my black work pants for weeks after they left. I had to buy one of those heavy-duty lint rollers, the ones with the really sticky tape? I found them on sale at Target. Target is so dangerous, though. You go in for a lint roller and come out with three throw pillows and a new coffee mug. They have this whole section right at the front with those dollar items, and they had these little ceramic pumpkins... \n\nBut, um, anyway. No. Even when she was here, and we were making the turkey and everything, I was just going through the motions. I haven't had a real break from feeling like this since it all started.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where these feelings of not wanting to wake up and losing interest in activities went away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It hasn't ever just... gone away. It's been pretty constant since that whole curriculum thing started. \n\nLike I said, there are days where I can push through it, especially when I'm at school and the kids are right there in front of me. They always have lunch right before my period, and the cafeteria serves these personal pan pizzas on Thursdays. The smell of that pizza grease just lingers in the hallway all afternoon. I used to love pizza day when I was a kid. My mom would give me a dollar to buy an extra slice, and I'd always get pepperoni. We had this little pizzeria in my hometown, Luigi's, and they had the best garlic knots. They'd wrap them in this foil paper that would be completely transparent with butter by the time you got them home. My dad used to joke that you could read a newspaper through the wrapper. He read the paper every morning, the actual physical paper, sitting at the kitchen table with his black coffee. He had this favorite mug that said \"World's Okayest Dad\" that I bought him for Father's Day when I was like... twelve? \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. No, the feeling hasn't gone away completely and come back. It's just... it's just always there, like a hum in the background that never stops.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, not even for two weeks. Like I was saying, it's been about a year and seven months of this, ever since I had to take over that STEM assessment redesign on top of all my regular classes.

No, definitely not two months. Honestly, it hasn't stopped at all. Like I mentioned before, this whole thing has been going on for about a year and seven months now, ever since I got stuck with that STEM assessment redesign while still teaching all my normal classes. 

It was just so much extra work, and the district didn't even give us a stipend for it. They just expected us to do it during our planning period. She was always better at holding her breath underwater than I was. She actually joined the swim team in high school and got a scholarship... 

Wait, what did you ask? Oh, right. Two months. No, I haven't had a break like that. It's just been the same for a year and seven months straight.

I... I feel like I just said this, didn't I? No, not two years. Like I mentioned a minute ago, it's been about a year and seven months. It all started right when I took over that STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load. 

I actually still have the binders for that project taking up half my dining room table. They're those massive three-inch D-ring binders. I had to buy them myself because the school only supplies the flimsy one-inch ones, and the rings on those always get misaligned. You know how they gap, and then every time you turn a page, the paper snags and tears? It drives me completely crazy. I used to get all my good office supplies at the Staples down the street, but they closed it last year and turned the building into one of those pop-up Spirit Halloween stores. It's so strange walking past it in August and seeing all these plastic skeletons and cobwebs in the window when it's ninety degrees outside. Dan actually hates Halloween. He says it's just a manufactured holiday for candy companies to make money. He won't even carve a pumpkin with me anymore because he hates the feeling of the pumpkin guts on his hands. He says the texture reminds him of... 

Wait, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. But no, to answer your question, not two years. Just the year and seven months.

I... are we going in circles? I feel like I keep saying the same thing, but no, not two weeks either. Like I've been saying, it hasn't stopped at all. It's been exactly the same for a year and seven months, ever since I took over that STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular classes.

I used to eat that every single morning when I was in middle school. You know how the milk gets all sugary and kind of gritty at the bottom of the bowl? I loved that. I can't really eat sugary cereal anymore, though, because my teeth are way too sensitive. I actually had to get a root canal a few months ago. The endodontist, Dr. Lin, he played this really loud smooth jazz the whole time. It was supposed to be relaxing, I guess, but it just made me feel like I was stuck in a hotel elevator while someone was drilling into my jaw. He did have this huge, beautiful fish tank in his waiting room, though, with all these little neon tetras. It all started around the time I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my regular teaching load. \n\nThey really needed someone to step up because the district completely overhauled the curriculum standards over the summer, and our science department chair, Brenda, went on maternity leave right before the school year started. She had a little girl, actually. Named her Hazel. I saw pictures on Facebook, and she's adorable, but Brenda's leave left us in such a bind. So the principal asked me to handle the redesign. I didn't really want to, but I have a hard time saying no, especially when it comes to the kids. And the new standards are just... they're so rigid. They want everything quantifiable, but how do you quantify a kid's curiosity when they're building a robotics project? We used to have this great unit where they'd design their own water filtration systems using recycled materials, and it was so hands-on, but now we have to spend three weeks prepping them for this standardized test that doesn't even measure practical application. It's just bubbling in scantrons. \n\nAnd the meetings... oh my gosh, the endless district meetings. They're always at the central office, which is all the way across town, right next to that new shopping center they just built. Have you seen it? They put in one of those fancy grocery stores where a bell pepper costs like four dollars. I went in there once just to grab a coffee, and the line was out the door. \n\nBut, um, anyway... yeah. A year and seven months. Ever since I took on that extra STEM project, everything just started feeling so heavy, and I lost interest in pretty much everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more years where you didn't feel this way, with interest in activities and without thoughts of not waking up?\n\nPatient: Wait, two years? Um... well, like I just said, this whole thing has been going on for about a year and seven months. Ever since I took over that STEM assessment redesign. So... before that, yeah, I guess I was fine. I wasn't having those thoughts or feeling this... blankness. \n\nBefore Brenda went on maternity leave, I actually really loved my job. I was running the after-school robotics club, and we were preparing for this regional competition over in the next county. It was at this huge convention center, the one they usually use for those big home and garden shows? My husband Dan and I went to one of those shows once, back when we were looking for ideas for our kitchen. They had this amazing booth with custom cabinetry made from reclaimed barn wood. The guy running the booth said he sourced all the wood from these old, dilapidated barns out in Amish country. He had this whole photo album showing the barns before they took them down. It was actually really sad, in a way, seeing these beautiful old structures just falling apart. \n\nBut anyway, the robotics competition was there, and my kids had built this little rover that could navigate a maze and pick up ping pong balls. They were so proud of it. We had these matching team t-shirts made, bright neon green so I wouldn't lose anyone in the crowd. I still have mine folded in the bottom of my dresser drawer. I haven't worn it since... well, since everything started feeling so heavy. \n\nSo, yeah. Before a year and seven months ago, I didn't feel like this. It really just started with that curriculum overhaul.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time, since this all started, where you felt completely back to normal for at least a couple of months?\n\nPatient: Um, completely back to normal for a couple of months? No, I don't think so. Not since it started. It's just been this constant weight. \n\nI mean, there are days where maybe I can fake it a little better, you know? Like when my sister came to visit for Thanksgiving. She drove all the way up from North Carolina. She brought her dog, this golden retriever mix named Buster. He is the sweetest thing, but he sheds everywhere. I swear I was finding blonde dog hair on my black work pants for weeks after they left. I had to buy one of those heavy-duty lint rollers, the ones with the really sticky tape? I found them on sale at Target. Target is so dangerous, though. You go in for a lint roller and come out with three throw pillows and a new coffee mug. They have this whole section right at the front with those dollar items, and they had these little ceramic pumpkins... \n\nBut, um, anyway. No. Even when she was here, and we were making the turkey and everything, I was just going through the motions. I haven't had a real break from feeling like this since it all started.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where these feelings of not wanting to wake up and losing interest in activities went away completely, only to come back later?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It hasn't ever just... gone away. It's been pretty constant since that whole curriculum thing started. \n\nLike I said, there are days where I can push through it, especially when I'm at school and the kids are right there in front of me. You kind of have to be \"on\" for them, you know? Especially my seventh graders. They have so much energy, especially right after lunch. They always have lunch right before my period, and the cafeteria serves these personal pan pizzas on Thursdays. The smell of that pizza grease just lingers in the hallway all afternoon. I used to love pizza day when I was a kid. My mom would give me a dollar to buy an extra slice, and I'd always get pepperoni. We had this little pizzeria in my hometown, Luigi's, and they had the best garlic knots. They'd wrap them in this foil paper that would be completely transparent with butter by the time you got them home. My dad used to joke that you could read a newspaper through the wrapper. He read the paper every morning, the actual physical paper, sitting at the kitchen table with his black coffee. He had this favorite mug that said \"World's Okayest Dad\" that I bought him for Father's Day when I was like... twelve? \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right. No, the feeling hasn't gone away completely and come back. It's just... it's just always there, like a hum in the background that never stops.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least two weeks since this all started?\n\nPatient: No, not even for two weeks. Like I was saying, it's been about a year and seven months of this, ever since I had to take over that STEM assessment redesign on top of all my regular classes. \n\nIt was just so much extra paperwork. I had to create all these new rubrics from scratch. I actually went to Staples to buy a new binder just to organize it all. I love office supplies, I always have. There's something about a brand new pack of pens, you know? I usually buy the Pilot G2s, the 0.5 millimeter ones, because the ink flows so nicely. Although, if you press too hard, they can bleed through the paper. I ruined a whole set of lesson plans once because I was pressing too hard. I was writing them at my dining room table... we have this beautiful oak table that Dan's grandfather made by hand. He was a carpenter, and he used to build all sorts of things. He even made a wooden rocking horse for Dan when he was a little boy. Dan's mom still has it in her attic. Attics always smell so dusty, don't they? Like old paper and mothballs. \n\nBut, um, no. Since I took on that redesign, it's just been a year and seven months of feeling like this, without any real break at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any distinct episodes of feeling normal, followed by a return of these thoughts and loss of interest?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. It's just been one long stretch. I haven't had any time where it goes away and then comes back. \n\nIt's kind of like... well, it's just always there. Like when you have a leaky faucet, you know? Tax season is just a nightmare for him. He practically lives at the office from February to April. He comes home smelling like stale coffee and dry-erase markers. I tried to fix the sink myself once. I got lost looking for the plumbing section and ended up looking at the patio furniture. They had this really nice wicker set with navy blue cushions... \n\nBut anyway, sorry, I'm rambling again. They just expected us to do it during our planning period. But my planning period is when I usually grade papers or make copies. The copy machine in the teacher's lounge is always broken, too. It's this ancient Xerox machine that jams if you even look at it funny. The paper gets stuck right in the middle, and you have to open all these little doors and pull out the crumpled, hot paper. It always gets toner on my hands. I try to wash it off in the staff bathroom, but they use that cheap pink soap that smells like public pools. Dan and I usually try to go up to Cape Cod for at least a week in July. His aunt has a little cottage near Yarmouth.     "functional_impairment": true,
```json
    "traumatic_stressor": null,
    "psychosocial_stressor": true,
    "additional_requirements": [
      "Onset prior to two years ago confirmed",
      "No distinct episodes of feeling normal, followed by a return of symptoms"
    ]
  }
}
```
